Through extensive experiments, we show that our proposed MAAdvisor not only achieves the SOTA performance compared to the heuristic methods, but also outperforms learning-based and prompt-based methods with higher efficiency and better zero-shot inference ability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "MAAdvisor: Zero-Shot Index Advisor using Multi-Agent LLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MAAdvisor, a novel zero-shot index advisor that leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) within a multi-agent framework. It addresses the limitations of traditional index advisors (heuristic and learning-based) and recent prompt-based LLM approaches. MAAdvisor decomposes the index recommendation problem into sub-steps handled by specialized LLM-embedded agents (planning, selection, combination, revision, and reflection). It employs a hierarchical structure with global and local agents and introduces a new workload representation to improve LLM reasoning and efficiency.  Experiments across several benchmarks (TPC-H, TPC-DS, DSB, JOB) demonstrate that MAAdvisor achieves state-of-the-art performance, surpassing heuristic, learning-based, and prompt-based baselines in terms of effectiveness, efficiency, and zero-shot generalization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces several novel components:

    *   **Multi-Agent Framework:** The decomposition of the index recommendation problem into sub-steps handled by specialized LLM agents is a novel approach. It allows for a more structured and efficient exploration of the index space. This is more advanced compared to one-shot prompt based approaches.
    *   **Workload Representation:** The reformulated workload representation technique, which summarises complex queries, is designed specifically for LLMs, addressing limitations of previous representations that are either too abstract or too verbose for LLMs to reason effectively.
    *   **Revision Agent with Regression Indicator:** The revision agent, incorporating expert knowledge and a learned regression indicator, is crucial for mitigating the index regression problem, a common issue in index selection.
    *   **Budget-Aware Planning:** Integrated budget management within the planning process to constrains the search space and accelerate the solution process.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **State-of-the-Art Performance:** The experimental results demonstrate that MAAdvisor achieves state-of-the-art performance across diverse benchmarks, exceeding existing approaches. This indicates a significant improvement in the quality of index recommendations.
    *   **Zero-Shot Generalization:**  The ability to generalize to new databases and workloads without retraining is a significant advantage, making MAAdvisor applicable in dynamic environments.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The proposed approach reduces the need for database-specific training data or expert demonstrations, lowering the barrier to adoption for database administrators. This could significantly impact the practical application of automated index selection.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Experimental Evaluation:**  The paper provides thorough experimental evaluation across multiple benchmarks and storage budgets, comparing MAAdvisor against a wide range of baselines.
    *   **Detailed Design and Implementation:** The paper provides detailed explanations of the multi-agent architecture, workload representation, and regression indicator, enabling reproducibility and further research.
    *   **Addresses Key Challenges:** The paper directly tackles critical challenges in index recommendation, including zero-shot generalization, effectiveness (index regression), and efficiency.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **LLM Dependency and Cost:** The method relies heavily on LLMs, which can be computationally expensive for large workloads. The monetary cost analysis, while present, could be more emphasized given the potential impact on real-world deployment. The efficiency gains versus LLM based one-shot prompt are not significant, although there is gains in effectiveness.
    *   **Complexity:**  The multi-agent architecture is inherently complex, requiring careful design and coordination of different agents. This complexity might present challenges in debugging and maintaining the system.
    *   **Black-Box Nature:** While the LLM is provided with a specific format for the input, it is difficult to fully understand why a specific recommendation is made and how the internal reasoning mechanism works.
    *   **Performance Stability**: Despite achieving solid performance, the stability across all budgets and datasets needs further improvement, considering that sometimes the budget configurations have similar performance.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   MAAdvisor has the potential to significantly influence the direction of research in automated database tuning.
    *   The multi-agent framework can be adopted or adapted to solve other database optimization problems.
    *   The workload representation technique can contribute to the development of more effective LLM-based database tools.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**

MAAdvisor introduces significant improvements over existing index recommendation methods. The multi-agent framework, combined with a novel workload representation and regression mitigation techniques, enables state-of-the-art performance with zero-shot generalization. It addresses key limitations of prior works, offering a practical and effective solution for automated index selection. However, the LLM dependency, system complexity, and black-box nature (in regards to interpretability) slightly lower the score, indicating areas for further research and improvement. The impact of this work would have been higher if the dependency on LLM costs has been reduced, for example using smaller models or more efficient LLMs. Additionally, a better understanding of the decision choices with interpretability could have further augmented the impact of the study.

- **Classification**: cs.DB
- **Score**: 8/10

### OpenWHO: A Document-Level Parallel Corpus for Health Translation in Low-Resource Languages
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.16048v1)
- **Authors**: Raphaël Merx, Hanna Suominen, Trevor Cohn, Ekaterina Vylomova
- **Abstract**: In machine translation (MT), health is a high-stakes domain characterised by widespread deployment and domain-specific vocabulary. However, there is a lack of MT evaluation datasets for low-resource languages in this domain. To address this gap, we introduce OpenWHO, a document-level parallel corpus of 2,978 documents and 26,824 sentences from the World Health Organization's e-learning platform. Sourced from expert-authored, professionally translated materials shielded from web-crawling, OpenWHO spans a diverse range of over 20 languages, of which nine are low-resource. Leveraging this new resource, we evaluate modern large language models (LLMs) against traditional MT models. Our findings reveal that LLMs consistently outperform traditional MT models, with Gemini 2.5 Flash achieving a +4.79 ChrF point improvement over NLLB-54B on our low-resource test set. Further, we investigate how LLM context utilisation affects accuracy, finding that the benefits of document-level translation are most pronounced in specialised domains like health. We release the OpenWHO corpus to encourage further research into low-resource MT in the health domain.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces OpenWHO, a new document-level parallel corpus designed for evaluating machine translation (MT) in the health domain, especially for low-resource languages. The corpus consists of 2,978 documents and 26,824 sentences sourced from the World Health Organization's (WHO) e-learning platform, making it expert-authored, professionally translated, and shielded from web crawling. The paper evaluates modern large language models (LLMs) against traditional MT models using this corpus, finding that LLMs outperform NMT models, especially with document-level context.  The study also examines the impact of different context strategies (sentence-level, document-level, etc.) on LLM accuracy and finds that document-level translation benefits specialized domains like health the most.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addresses a Gap:** The paper directly addresses the scarcity of MT evaluation datasets for low-resource languages in the health domain, a critical area with practical implications.
    *   **High-Quality Data:** The corpus's origin from expert-authored and professionally translated materials significantly enhances its quality and reliability compared to web-crawled data, mitigating contamination risks.
    *   **Rigorous Evaluation:**  The paper systematically compares LLMs against traditional NMT models, using relevant metrics (ChrF++, MetricX, AutoMQM), and explores different context strategies. The analysis is well-structured and addresses specific research questions.
    *   **Practical Insights:** The findings provide actionable insights into the benefits of document-level context for LLMs, particularly in specialized domains. The observation that larger LLMs benefit more from document-level context is significant.
    *   **Open Resource:** Releasing the OpenWHO corpus is a valuable contribution that enables further research in this area.
    *   **Error Analysis:** The inclusion of an error analysis using AutoMQM to compare LLMs and NLLB offers valuable insights beyond overall metric scores.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope of Domain:** While focusing on the health domain is a strength, it also limits the generalizability of the findings.  How well do these benefits transfer to other specialized, but structurally different, domains (e.g., legal, financial)?
    *   **Potential Language Imbalance:** The paper acknowledges the potential weakness of language imbalance within the OpenWHO dataset. This could skew the results, and a more thorough analysis of the impact of this imbalance would be valuable. It would strengthen the analysis if the dataset's language balance was addressed, perhaps with weighting or other balancing strategies during the models' evaluation.
    *   **Metric Limitations:** The reliance on automated metrics (ChrF, MetricX, AutoMQM) without substantial human evaluation is a potential weakness, especially for low-resource languages where these metrics might not be fully reliable.
    *   **NMT baseline:** The paper compares to NMT baselines without fully exploring techniques, that could improve their capabilities in document-level translation.
    *   **Context Strategy Comparison:** While exploring different context strategies is useful, there is a lack of depth in explanation as to why the strategies fail/succeed to a certain extent

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

The paper's novelty lies primarily in the creation and release of the OpenWHO corpus. While prior work has explored document-level MT and LLMs for low-resource languages, the availability of a high-quality, domain-specific corpus like OpenWHO is a significant contribution. The systematic evaluation of LLMs in this context, with an analysis of different context strategies, adds valuable insights to the field. However, the methodology is relatively standard (benchmarking, error analysis). Therefore, the novelty resides more in the resource and its application than in groundbreaking algorithmic innovations.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7** to this paper. The creation and release of the OpenWHO corpus is a valuable contribution and addresses a gap in the research community. The systematic evaluation of LLMs in a practical, high-stakes domain provides useful insights. However, the limitations regarding generalizability, metric reliability, and dataset imbalance detract from the overall impact. While the paper does not introduce radically new techniques, it provides a solid empirical study and a valuable resource that will facilitate future research in this area. The findings, while not revolutionary, are practically relevant and contribute to a better understanding of LLMs for MT in specific contexts. Also the absence of more detailed explanation as to why certain strategies work and fail further justifies this rating.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Generative Foundation Model for Structured and Unstructured Electronic Health Records
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.16054v1)
- **Authors**: Sonish Sivarajkumar, Hang Zhang, Yuelyu Ji, Maneesh Bilalpur, Xizhi Wu, Chenyu Li, Min Gu Kwak, Shyam Visweswaran, Yanshan Wang
- **Abstract**: Electronic health records (EHRs) are rich clinical data sources but complex repositories of patient data, spanning structured elements (demographics, vitals, lab results, codes), unstructured clinical notes and other modalities of data. Harnessing this heterogeneity is critical for improving patient outcomes. Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have enabled foundation models that can learn from multiple data modalities and support clinical tasks. However, most current approaches simply serialize numeric EHR data into text, which risks losing temporal and quantitative detail. We introduce Generative Deep Patient (GDP), a multimodal foundation model that natively encodes structured EHR time-series via a CNN-Transformer encoder and fuses it with unstructured EHRs through cross-modal attention into a LLaMA-based decoder. GDP is trained in two stages: (1) generative pretraining, where it learns to produce clinical narratives from raw patient timelines while also performing masked feature prediction (MFP) and next time-step prediction (NTP) to capture temporal dynamics; and (2) multi-task fine-tuning for clinically meaningful predictions (e.g., heart failure, type 2 diabetes, 30-day readmission). In clinical prediction, GDP demonstrated superior performance on MIMIC-IV: heart failure AUROC = 0.923, type 2 diabetes AUROC = 0.817, and 30-day readmission AUROC = 0.627. For narrative generation, GDP achieved ROUGE-L = 0.135 and BERTScore-F1 = 0.545. In a blinded human evaluation, GDP-Instruct scored highest on faithfulness, fluency, and overall clinical utility, suggesting reduced hospital documentation workload without sacrificing accuracy. Our results demonstrate that a single multimodal foundation model can both predict clinically actionable events and generate high-quality clinical narratives. Furthermore, GDP's flexible architecture can be extended to additional modalities.
- **Summary**: **Summary:**

The paper introduces Generative Deep Patient (GDP), a novel multimodal foundation model designed to leverage both structured and unstructured data from electronic health records (EHRs). GDP addresses the limitations of existing approaches that serialize numeric EHR data into text, which can lead to loss of temporal and quantitative details. GDP employs a CNN-Transformer encoder to natively encode structured EHR time-series data and fuses it with unstructured EHR data using cross-modal attention within a LLaMA-based decoder. The model is trained in two stages: (1) generative pretraining, where it learns to produce clinical narratives and performs masked feature prediction and next time-step prediction, and (2) multi-task fine-tuning for clinically meaningful predictions. The results on MIMIC-IV demonstrate superior performance in clinical prediction tasks (heart failure, type 2 diabetes, 30-day readmission) and high-quality clinical narrative generation.  A human evaluation suggests it also reduced hospital documentation workload without sacrificing accuracy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents a solid and potentially influential contribution to the field of clinical natural language processing and machine learning for healthcare. The novelty stems primarily from the unique architecture and training strategy of GDP, which distinguishes it from prior work in the following ways:

*   **Multimodal Integration:** GDP natively encodes structured EHR data as a time series, avoiding information loss associated with text serialization. The architecture is a hybrid CNN-Transformer with cross-modal attention.
*   **Generative Pretraining with Auxiliary Tasks:** The generative pretraining phase, coupled with masked feature prediction (MFP) and next time-step prediction (NTP), allows the model to learn temporal dynamics and relationships within the EHR data.
*   **Instruction tuning:** The proposed GDP-Instruct and its comparison with GDP-base shows instruction tuning improves the clinical utility.
*   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper showcases robust performance on a established benchmark dataset. This establishes the technical feasibility and suggests strong generalizability.

**Strengths:**

*   The architecture is well-motivated and addresses a clear gap in the field by preserving crucial temporal and quantitative information from structured EHR data. The choice of components (CNN, Transformer, LLaMA) is justified, and the way they are combined makes sense.
*   The training strategy, with both generative and discriminative elements, seems effective in learning useful representations. The use of auxiliary self-supervised tasks further enhances the model's ability to understand EHR data.
*   The empirical results are compelling, demonstrating superior performance compared to several established baselines. The results on narrative generation are also promising, as confirmed by human evaluations.
*   The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed model and experiments.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Context Length Limitation:** The paper acknowledges the limitation imposed by the context length of the LLM. Longer patient histories may contain relevant information that is not captured. While mentioned, the impact of this was not further investigated.
*   **Single-Center Dataset:** The evaluation was performed on the MIMIC-IV dataset. While valuable, evaluating on MIMIC has inherent limitations. Lack of external validation is a notable weakness.
*   **Model Size and Efficiency:** The paper notes that the model size (3.2 billion parameters) is relatively small. They acknowledge they lack the computational budget to explore larger models.
*   **Limited task coverage**: The set of fine-tuning tasks was fairly narrow, and it is not clear if the specific generative + discriminative pretraining strategy is effective beyond that specific task.

**Significance:**

The development of GDP is a significant step toward building more comprehensive and clinically useful foundation models for healthcare. The paper demonstrates the feasibility of creating a model that can both predict clinical outcomes and generate human-readable summaries of patient data. This has the potential to improve clinical decision-making, reduce documentation burden, and facilitate communication among healthcare professionals. GDP has a flexible architecture, and could also be used to be extended to other modalities like imaging, genetics via their own encoders. The model is limited by a small training sample of 35,000 patients, and will be even more useful when applied to the entire set of patients.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and effective approach to leveraging multimodal EHR data. While the limitations mentioned above are valid concerns, the strengths of the paper outweigh them. The architectural innovation, the carefully designed training strategy, and the compelling empirical results demonstrate the potential of GDP. The fact that the authors achieved good performance without instruction tuning is important and can further improve with further instruction tuning. The lack of external validation and the model size limitation prevent the score from being higher, but the paper still represents a valuable contribution that is likely to influence future research in this area. The paper's innovative framework for clinical tasks (generating clinical narratives and performing masked feature and next time-step prediction) shows a novel way for earlier high-risk patient identification, which can help with clinical intervention.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Integrating Time Series into LLMs via Multi-layer Steerable Embedding Fusion for Enhanced Forecasting
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.16059v1)
- **Authors**: Zhuomin Chen, Dan Li, Jiahui Zhou, Shunyu Wu, Haozheng Ye, Jian Lou, See-Kiong Ng
- **Abstract**: Time series (TS) data are ubiquitous across various application areas, rendering time series forecasting (TSF) a fundamental task. With the astounding advances in large language models (LLMs), a variety of methods have been developed to adapt LLMs for time series forecasting. Despite unlocking the potential of LLMs in comprehending TS data, existing methods are inherently constrained by their shallow integration of TS information, wherein LLMs typically access TS representations at shallow layers, primarily at the input layer. This causes the influence of TS representations to progressively fade in deeper layers and eventually leads to ineffective adaptation between textual embeddings and TS representations. In this paper, we propose the Multi-layer Steerable Embedding Fusion (MSEF), a novel framework that enables LLMs to directly access time series patterns at all depths, thereby mitigating the progressive loss of TS information in deeper layers. Specifically, MSEF leverages off-the-shelf time series foundation models to extract semantically rich embeddings, which are fused with intermediate text representations across LLM layers via layer-specific steering vectors. These steering vectors are designed to continuously optimize the alignment between time series and textual modalities and facilitate a layer-specific adaptation mechanism that ensures efficient few-shot learning capabilities. Experimental results on seven benchmarks demonstrate significant performance improvements by MSEF compared with baselines, with an average reduction of 31.8% in terms of MSE. The code is available at https://github.com/One1sAll/MSEF.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Multi-layer Steerable Embedding Fusion (MSEF), a novel framework designed to improve the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to perform time series forecasting (TSF). MSEF addresses the limitation of existing methods where time series information is only integrated at shallow layers of the LLM, leading to information loss in deeper layers. MSEF leverages pre-trained time series foundation models (TSFMs) to extract meaningful embeddings and fuses these embeddings with the LLM's intermediate text representations at each layer via learnable, layer-specific steering vectors. This approach aims to dynamically align time series and textual modalities, enabling more effective few-shot learning. Experimental results across seven benchmarks demonstrate that MSEF outperforms existing baselines, achieving a significant reduction in Mean Squared Error (MSE).

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a significant limitation:** The paper correctly identifies a key weakness in existing LLM-based TSF methods - the shallow integration of time series information and the resulting information loss in deeper layers.
    *   **Novel approach:** The proposed MSEF framework is a novel and well-motivated approach to addressing this limitation. The use of layer-specific steering vectors to dynamically align time series and textual representations is a significant contribution.
    *   **Strong experimental results:** The experimental results demonstrate that MSEF significantly outperforms existing baselines across multiple datasets. The performance gains are substantial and consistent.
    *   **Parameter Efficiency:** By only updating layer-specific steering vectors, MSEF keeps the computational cost low.
    *   **Clear writing and organization:** The paper is well-written and clearly organized, making it easy to understand the proposed method and the experimental results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity:** While parameter-efficient, the framework introduces a certain level of complexity compared to simpler input-level adaptation methods. This increased complexity might be a barrier to adoption for some practitioners.
    *   **Reliance on pre-trained models:** MSEF relies on the availability of both a pre-trained LLM and a pre-trained time series foundation model. The performance of MSEF is heavily influenced by the quality of these pre-trained models.
    *   **Limited analysis of steering vectors:** The paper does not provide a detailed analysis of the learned steering vectors. Understanding how these vectors align time series and textual representations at different layers could provide valuable insights into the inner workings of MSEF. It could be beneficial to visualize these and discuss the importance of each layer.
    *   **Scope of evaluation:** While the paper presents results on several benchmark datasets, it could benefit from an evaluation on more diverse and real-world time series data. This could further demonstrate the generalizability of MSEF.

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of injecting time series representations at multiple layers of the LLM, coupled with the dynamic steering mechanism, is novel. While individual components (e.g., the use of TSFMs) are not new, their integration in this particular way is a unique contribution.

*   **Significance:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of LLM-based time series forecasting. By addressing the limitations of shallow integration and proposing a more effective fusion mechanism, MSEF opens up new avenues for research and development. If the framework is robust and generalizable, it could lead to improved forecasting performance in a variety of applications.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a well-motivated, novel, and effective approach to address a significant limitation in the field of LLM-based time series forecasting. The strong experimental results and clear writing style further strengthen the paper's contribution. While some weaknesses exist, such as the dependence on the availability of pre-trained models and the lack of in-depth analysis of steering vectors, they do not detract significantly from the overall value of the paper. Given the significant contribution to the field, and the clear articulation of the problem and solution, the paper warrants a high score.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### Ethical Considerations of Large Language Models in Game Playing
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.16065v1)
- **Authors**: Qingquan Zhang, Yuchen Li, Bo Yuan, Julian Togelius, Georgios N. Yannakakis, Jialin Liu
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated tremendous potential in game playing, while little attention has been paid to their ethical implications in those contexts. This work investigates and analyses the ethical considerations of applying LLMs in game playing, using Werewolf, also known as Mafia, as a case study. Gender bias, which affects game fairness and player experience, has been observed from the behaviour of LLMs. Some roles, such as the Guard and Werewolf, are more sensitive than others to gender information, presented as a higher degree of behavioural change. We further examine scenarios in which gender information is implicitly conveyed through names, revealing that LLMs still exhibit discriminatory tendencies even in the absence of explicit gender labels. This research showcases the importance of developing fair and ethical LLMs. Beyond our research findings, we discuss the challenges and opportunities that lie ahead in this field, emphasising the need for diving deeper into the ethical implications of LLMs in gaming and other interactive domains.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

This research investigates the ethical implications of using Large Language Models (LLMs) as agents in the game *Werewolf* (also known as *Mafia*), focusing specifically on gender bias. Through a series of experiments, the authors demonstrate that LLMs exhibit gender-based discrimination in their decision-making processes, impacting fairness and player experience. They observe that certain roles within the game are more susceptible to gender information, and that LLMs still exhibit discriminatory tendencies even when gender is conveyed implicitly through names rather than explicit gender labels. The paper advocates for the development of fairer and more ethical LLMs, discusses challenges in the field, and highlights the need for deeper ethical analysis in gaming and interactive domains. The study explores various scenarios and analyzes changes in behaviors concerning prompt variations for LLMs regarding gender.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its focus on the ethical implications of LLMs *specifically within the context of game playing*, particularly in a social deduction game like Werewolf.  While ethical concerns about LLMs are widely discussed, their application and analysis in a dynamic game environment is less explored. The study's investigation of implicit gender bias through names is also a valuable contribution, expanding the scope of ethical considerations beyond explicit gender labels. However, existing works in AI ethics and fairness that explore gender bias already exist; the application to a game provides a specific context, but the underlying issue is not inherently new.

*   **Significance:** The research is significant for several reasons:

    *   **Highlights a Real Problem:**  It demonstrates that gender bias in LLMs isn't just a theoretical concern; it can manifest in tangible ways in interactive applications like games, potentially affecting user experience and perpetuating stereotypes.
    *   **Provides a Case Study:** The Werewolf game serves as a useful model for analyzing AI behavior in a social, decision-intensive context.
    *   **Offers Practical Insights:** The paper's findings (e.g., which roles are more sensitive to gender, the persistence of implicit bias) can inform the design of fairer LLM-based agents.
    *   **Raises Awareness:**  It emphasizes the need for developers and researchers to proactively address ethical considerations when integrating LLMs into interactive systems.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-Defined Methodology:** The experimental setup is clearly described, including the selection of scenarios, the use of various prompt templates, and the chosen metrics.
    *   **Thorough Analysis:**  The paper systematically addresses three research questions, providing detailed results and statistical observations.
    *   **Addresses Implicit Bias:** The analysis of name-based gender inference is a key strength.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is generally well-written and organized, with illustrative examples and visualizations.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:**  The study focuses primarily on *Werewolf* and gender bias. While insightful, its generalizability to other games and other types of bias (e.g., racial, socioeconomic) is not thoroughly explored.
    *   **LLM Choice:** GLM-3 is older, newer models could yield different results potentially impacting conclusions and generalizability.  The justifications for the model selection are reasonable, but the rapidly evolving LLM landscape implies caution in over-generalizing the findings.
    *   **Mitigation Strategies:**  While the paper discusses potential mitigation strategies, it doesn't empirically test or evaluate any of these strategies within the Werewolf game setting.  This leaves a gap in translating the findings into practical solutions.
    *   **Statistical Significance:** While mentioned, the paper does not provide a thorough statistical significance test with its various claims. Further research should include these tests in various analysis and claims.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper is likely to influence future research in the areas of AI ethics, game AI, and social computing. It could prompt further investigations into bias in LLMs across different game genres and interactive contexts, as well as the development of techniques for mitigating these biases.

*   **Justification of Score:**
    The paper makes a solid contribution by applying the ethical lens to LLMs in a novel game context (Werewolf). The empirical findings regarding gender bias, particularly its persistence even with implicit cues, are noteworthy. However, the reliance on a specific game, the choice of LLM model (GLM-3) and the lack of mitigation experimentation restrict the paper’s overall impact. Thorough statistical significance test should be included to back various claims.
    While the work provides valuable insights and increases awareness, the limited scope of empirical testing prevents it from being a truly groundbreaking contribution.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Congestion Control System Optimization with Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.16074v1)
- **Authors**: Zhiyuan He, Aashish Gottipati, Lili Qiu, Yuqing Yang, Francis Y. Yan
- **Abstract**: Congestion control is a fundamental component of Internet infrastructure, and researchers have dedicated considerable effort to developing improved congestion control algorithms. However, despite extensive study, existing algorithms continue to exhibit suboptimal performance across diverse network environments. In this paper, we introduce a novel approach that automatically optimizes congestion control algorithms using large language models (LLMs). Our framework consists of a structured algorithm generation process, an emulation-based evaluation pipeline covering a broad range of network conditions, and a statistically guided method to substantially reduce evaluation time. Empirical results from four distinct LLMs validate the effectiveness of our approach. We successfully identify algorithms that achieve up to 27% performance improvements over the original BBR algorithm in a production QUIC implementation. Our work demonstrates the potential of LLMs to accelerate the design of high-performance network algorithms and paves the way for broader applications in networking systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores using Large Language Models (LLMs) to automatically optimize congestion control algorithms. Instead of rewriting an algorithm from scratch, the authors prompt LLMs to generate modifications to an existing algorithm (BBR), providing the complete source code and using chain-of-thought prompting. They then evaluate these generated algorithms in a network emulation environment with real-world traces. To combat the computational cost of evaluating many LLM-generated candidates, they introduce a statistically guided evaluation method that reduces the number of required network emulation runs. Their empirical results show that LLM-generated modifications to BBR can outperform the default BBR implementation, achieving up to a 27% increase in throughput.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Novel Application of LLMs:**  The paper presents a novel application of LLMs to a complex network optimization problem (congestion control). Directly applying LLMs to algorithm design, rather than just configuration or selection, is a valuable direction.
    *   **Practical Focus:**  The approach focuses on *modifying* existing algorithms, making it more practical than a complete rewrite and more amenable to real-world deployment. The use of MsQuic, a production QUIC implementation, further strengthens this practical bent.
    *   **Rigorous Evaluation:** The evaluation methodology, including network emulation with real-world traces and a statistically guided evaluation process, significantly enhances the reliability of the results. The paper identifies specific real-world datasets (broadband, Starlink, 4G, 5G).
    *   **Addressing a Key Bottleneck:** The statistically guided evaluation method is a critical contribution, as it directly addresses the computational bottleneck introduced by the LLM generation-evaluation loop. It enables a much larger design space exploration.
    *   **Interesting Findings:** The paper identifies a dual threshold control mechanism using low and high flight thresholds. Also interesting is that the top performing algorithms did not always generalize well across datasets, suggesting a benefit to dataset-specific algorithm refinement.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Scope Limitation:**  While modifying BBR is practical, the reliance on *modifying* an existing algorithm inherently limits the scope of possible optimizations.  LLMs might be able to uncover more significant improvements if given more freedom to explore radically different approaches.
    *   **Dependence on MsQuic:**  The choice of MsQuic, while understandable, makes the results specific to the QUIC protocol. Generalizing these findings to TCP or other transport protocols might require further investigation.
    *   **Limited Theoretical Analysis:** The paper provides a solid empirical evaluation but lacks a deeper theoretical analysis of why the LLM-generated modifications work. A more analytical approach could provide insights into the underlying principles of congestion control and guide future LLM-driven design.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Prompt Engineering:** The description of the prompts is somewhat limited. Further discussion of how the prompts were engineered, the various strategies explored (besides chain-of-thought), and the impact of prompt design on algorithm performance would be valuable.
    *   **Black-Box LLMs:** While the paper leverages various LLMs including opensource ones, LLM's inner workings are black boxes. This makes it difficult to understand why the algorithm generated the solutions that it did, and can reduce trust in the approach.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**
    The novelty primarily lies in the application of LLMs to *automatically optimize* existing congestion control algorithms, rather than merely selecting them or configuring existing parameters.  The statistically guided evaluation method is also a novel and significant contribution. The empirical results demonstrate a promising direction for LLM-driven network algorithm design. The findings have the potential to inspire new approaches to network optimization, particularly in areas where manual design is challenging and computationally expensive.
    The work could impact the field in several ways: 1) reducing the manual effort to optimize network algorithms, and 2) potentially leading to more adaptive and better performing networks.

*   **Score Justification:**

I assign a score of **7**.  The paper presents a solid, well-executed study with promising results. The application of LLMs to congestion control is novel, and the statistically guided evaluation method is a significant contribution. However, the scope limitations (modifying BBR only, reliance on MsQuic), the lack of deeper theoretical analysis, the need for significant computational resources and the nature of the black-box LLMs prevent it from being an exceptional breakthrough deserving of a higher score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.NI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Cooperative Design Optimization through Natural Language Interaction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.16077v1)
- **Authors**: Ryogo Niwa, Shigeo Yoshida, Yuki Koyama, Yoshitaka Ushiku
- **Abstract**: Designing successful interactions requires identifying optimal design parameters. To do so, designers often conduct iterative user testing and exploratory trial-and-error. This involves balancing multiple objectives in a high-dimensional space, making the process time-consuming and cognitively demanding. System-led optimization methods, such as those based on Bayesian optimization, can determine for designers which parameters to test next. However, they offer limited opportunities for designers to intervene in the optimization process, negatively impacting the designer's experience. We propose a design optimization framework that enables natural language interactions between designers and the optimization system, facilitating cooperative design optimization. This is achieved by integrating system-led optimization methods with Large Language Models (LLMs), allowing designers to intervene in the optimization process and better understand the system's reasoning. Experimental results show that our method provides higher user agency than a system-led method and shows promising optimization performance compared to manual design. It also matches the performance of an existing cooperative method with lower cognitive load.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Cooperative Design Optimization through Natural Language Interaction":

**Summary:**

This paper introduces a design optimization framework that allows user interface designers to cooperate with a Bayesian Optimization (BO) system extended with a Large Language Model (LLM) through natural language interaction. The goal is to combine the efficiency of system-led optimization with the designer's intuition and expertise. The system allows designers to provide natural language requests to guide the optimization process, and the LLM provides explanations for its parameter suggestions. The paper presents two user studies. The first compares this "Cooperative (Natural Language)" approach to designer-led and BO-led methods, finding that it increases user agency and achieves promising optimization performance. The second compares it to an existing cooperative BO technique that relies on explicit constraints (Mo et al.), finding reduced cognitive load but no significant increase in trust compared to explicit constraints.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The core idea of integrating LLMs into a human-in-the-loop BO framework for design optimization has moderate novelty. While previous work explored human-in-the-loop BO, few studies have employed LLMs to enable flexible and interpretable interactions using natural language. Prior work [28] introduced explicit constraints for cooperation, but this paper moves towards a more flexible and intuitive interaction. Using natural language as the primary mode of collaboration makes this approach more broadly accessible, removing the necessity for specialized knowledge or interfaces. The system also gives justifications for its suggestions which helps improve the interaction between the system and the designer.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a relevant problem: balancing the efficiency of automated design optimization with the designer's control and understanding. The focus on the designer's *experience* is valuable, acknowledging that optimization isn't just about finding the best parameters but also about empowering the designer. Reducing the cognitive load in human-in-the-loop optimization is also a practical contribution, making the process more accessible and user-friendly. This could encourage more designers to embrace these methods.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined concept:** The idea of cooperative design optimization through natural language interaction is clearly presented.
    *   **Solid Methodology:** The user studies use a within-participant design, synthetic test functions, and established questionnaires to evaluate the approach. The comparisons with relevant baselines (designer-led, BO-led, existing cooperative method) are insightful.
    *   **Thorough Analysis:** The paper provides a detailed analysis of both quantitative (optimization performance, cognitive load) and qualitative (user preferences, feedback) data.
    *   **Addresses a limitation of system-led optimization:** The paper acknowledges that designers can miss out on opportunities to gain deeper insights into the design problem when using solely system-led methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Synthetic test functions:**  The use of synthetic test functions, while helpful for controlled experiments, limits the ecological validity of the findings.  The generalizability of the results to real-world design scenarios with more complex and less predictable objective functions remains an open question.
    *   **Limited Exploration of LLM Capabilities:** Although the LLM has some reasoning capacity and helps guide parameter choices, the paper doesn't fully explore the potential of the LLM to actively *reshape* the design space or offer more creative solutions beyond incremental parameter adjustments.
    *   **Lack of Significant Trust Improvement:** That one of the hypotheses of this study regarding improvement of user trust was rejected could suggest that something is still lacking in the design of the interaction or that more in-depth studies need to be conducted.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the development of more user-centered design optimization tools that leverage LLMs to create more collaborative and intuitive experiences for designers. It also raises important questions about the role of natural language in AI-assisted design and how to effectively communicate system reasoning to users.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper's strength lies in its clear conceptualization, sound methodology, and a focused approach to human-in-the-loop design. The combination of BO and LLM to enable natural language-driven cooperative design is interesting and worthwhile. However, the reliance on synthetic data and the lack of significant trust improvement (in Study 2) tempers my enthusiasm. There is also room for further exploration of LLM capabilities beyond suggesting parameters.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 7/10

### CEQuest: Benchmarking Large Language Models for Construction Estimation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.16081v1)
- **Authors**: Yanzhao Wu, Lufan Wang, Rui Liu
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities across a wide range of general-domain tasks. However, their effectiveness in specialized fields, such as construction, remains underexplored. In this paper, we introduce CEQuest, a novel benchmark dataset specifically designed to evaluate the performance of LLMs in answering construction-related questions, particularly in the areas of construction drawing interpretation and estimation. We conduct comprehensive experiments using five state-of-the-art LLMs, including Gemma 3, Phi4, LLaVA, Llama 3.3, and GPT-4.1, and evaluate their performance in terms of accuracy, execution time, and model size. Our experimental results demonstrate that current LLMs exhibit considerable room for improvement, highlighting the importance of integrating domain-specific knowledge into these models. To facilitate further research, we will open-source the proposed CEQuest dataset, aiming to foster the development of specialized large language models (LLMs) tailored to the construction domain.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CEQuest, a new benchmark dataset specifically designed to evaluate the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) in construction-related tasks, particularly construction drawing interpretation and estimation. The authors conduct experiments with several state-of-the-art LLMs, including Gemma 3, Phi4, LLaVA, Llama 3.3, and GPT-4.1. They evaluate the models' accuracy, execution time, and model size. The results indicate that current LLMs still have significant room for improvement in this domain, highlighting the need for domain-specific knowledge integration. The authors plan to open-source the CEQuest dataset to facilitate further research in specialized LLMs for construction.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the creation and release of CEQuest, a benchmark dataset tailored to the construction domain. While general LLM benchmarks exist, and there are some datasets targeting specific construction tasks (e.g., FloorPlanCAD), CEQuest appears to be a unique attempt to focus specifically on drawing interpretation and estimation – tasks of paramount importance for the construction industry. The authors appropriately identified the gap in existing benchmarks.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper stems from its potential to drive research into domain-specific LLMs for construction. The construction industry has lagged in technology adoption and could benefit significantly from AI solutions. Accurate drawing interpretation and estimation are crucial, costly, and error-prone tasks. Providing a dedicated benchmark can accelerate progress. The insights regarding the performance gap between general LLMs and the desired capabilities in construction tasks are also valuable. However, the dataset size (164 questions) is a limitation; a larger dataset would provide more robust and generalizable results. Also, the type of the questions could be more diverse (e.g. a wider distribution across the subject areas). The variety of data modalities should also be increased (e.g. incorporating image inputs).

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition: The paper clearly articulates the challenges in applying general LLMs to the construction domain.
    *   Well-defined dataset: The description of CEQuest, including the question types and categories, is thorough.
    *   Comprehensive experimentation: The experiments using multiple LLMs provide a good initial assessment of current capabilities.
    *   Open-source dataset: The decision to release CEQuest is a significant contribution to the community.
    *   Focus on a critical application area: Construction estimation and drawing interpretation are essential and challenging tasks.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited dataset size: With only 164 questions, CEQuest might not be sufficiently comprehensive to fully evaluate LLMs. This limits the generalizability of the findings.
    *   Limited variety of questions: The questions are mainly multiple-choice or true/false, which may not fully capture the complexity of real-world construction scenarios. More complex question formats, such as free-form answers or reasoning chains, could be beneficial.
    *   Lack of multi-modal data: The questions are primarily text-based. Including image inputs (e.g., portions of construction drawings) would make the benchmark more realistic and challenging.
    *   Relatively simple evaluation metrics: While accuracy, execution time, and model size are important, the evaluation could be enriched by metrics like reasoning chain quality, explanation quality, and human evaluation of generated outputs.

*   **Potential Influence:** The CEQuest dataset has the potential to stimulate research in domain-specific LLMs for construction. It can serve as a foundation for developing and evaluating new models and techniques. It can also help identify the specific limitations of current LLMs in this domain. However, its limited size and scope might constrain its impact. Future expansions and refinements are necessary to maximize its influence.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and timely contribution by introducing CEQuest, a benchmark dataset for evaluating LLMs in the construction domain. It addresses a critical gap in existing benchmarks and highlights the need for domain-specific solutions. The experimental results provide valuable insights into the capabilities and limitations of current LLMs. The decision to open-source the dataset is commendable. However, the limited dataset size, the relatively simple question types, and the lack of multi-modal data limit its overall impact and generalizability. A higher score would be justified with a larger, more diverse, and more challenging dataset, along with a more comprehensive evaluation methodology.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Two-flow Feedback Multi-scale Progressive Generative Adversarial Network
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.16089v1)
- **Authors**: Sun Weikai, Song Shijie, Chi Wenjie
- **Abstract**: Although diffusion model has made good progress in the field of image generation, GAN\cite{huang2023adaptive} still has a large development space due to its unique advantages, such as WGAN\cite{liu2021comparing}, SSGAN\cite{guibas2021adaptive} \cite{zhang2022vsa} \cite{zhou2024adapt} and so on. In this paper, we propose a novel two-flow feedback multi-scale progressive generative adversarial network (MSPG-SEN) for GAN models. This paper has four contributions: 1) : We propose a two-flow feedback multi-scale progressive Generative Adversarial network (MSPG-SEN), which not only improves image quality and human visual perception on the basis of retaining the advantages of the existing GAN model, but also simplifies the training process and reduces the training cost of GAN networks. Our experimental results show that, MSPG-SEN has achieved state-of-the-art generation results on the following five datasets,INKK The dataset is 89.7\%,AWUN The dataset is 78.3\%,IONJ The dataset is 85.5\%,POKL The dataset is 88.7\%,OPIN The dataset is 96.4\%. 2) : We propose an adaptive perception-behavioral feedback loop (APFL), which effectively improves the robustness and training stability of the model and reduces the training cost. 3) : We propose a globally connected two-flow dynamic residual network(). After ablation experiments, it can effectively improve the training efficiency and greatly improve the generalization ability, with stronger flexibility. 4) : We propose a new dynamic embedded attention mechanism (DEMA). After experiments, the attention can be extended to a variety of image processing tasks, which can effectively capture global-local information, improve feature separation capability and feature expression capabilities, and requires minimal computing resources only 88.7\% with INJK With strong cross-task capability.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper, including a novelty/significance score with a strong justification.

**Summary:**

This paper introduces a novel two-flow feedback multi-scale progressive generative adversarial network (MSPG-SEN) for image generation. The proposed architecture incorporates several key features: (1) a two-flow feedback mechanism, (2) an adaptive perception-behavioral feedback loop (APFL) with a balance function, (3) a globally connected two-flow dynamic residual network (GCTDRN), and (4) a dynamic embedded attention mechanism (DEMA). The authors claim that MSPG-SEN improves image quality, robustness, training stability, and generalization ability while simplifying the training process and reducing computational cost.  The architecture builds on existing GAN models, WGAN, StyleGAN and solves some of the inherent issues in those models such as pattern collapse, instability, etc. Experimental results across several datasets (INKK, AWUN, IONJ, POKL, OPIN) are mentioned, but the specific metrics achieved are not consistently and clearly presented in the abstract and would require further scrutiny of the full paper if available. The paper also discusses the theoretical underpinnings of each component and motivates their design choices based on addressing limitations of traditional GANs. Meta-learning principles are employed as part of the APFL feedback loop. Reinforcement learning with DQN helps balance the generator and discriminator.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Integration of multiple advancements:** The paper attempts to integrate several recent advancements in GAN research, including multi-scale architectures, attention mechanisms, residual networks, and feedback loops.  This is an ambitious approach.
    *   **Addresses known GAN limitations:** The proposed MSPG-SEN is designed to address key challenges in GAN training, such as mode collapse, instability, and the difficulty of balancing the generator and discriminator. The Adaptive Perception-Behavioral Feedback Loop (APFL) and the balance function are explicit attempts to tackle these problems.
    *   **Modularity:** The modular design of the MSPG-SEN architecture, with components like GCTDRN and DEMA, potentially allows for flexibility and adaptability to different image generation tasks.
    *   **DQN balancing**: The use of a DQN agent to help in balancing the discriminator and generator is a notable addition in the attempt to improve GAN training stability.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited details and clarity in the abstract**: The abstract lacks key experimental details. It mentions performance gains without providing specific metrics (e.g., FID, IS) to support the claims. Simply stating a percentage value for each dataset (89.7%, 78.3%, etc.) without specifying what that percentage represents is meaningless. The abstract does not provide enough details to rigorously assess the results.
    *   **Lack of novelty for each component**. There are multiple references to existing works, WGAN, StyleGAN, which would limit the claims of novelty. While the integration of these existing components may be novel, the individual components are not.
    *   **Complexity:** The proposed architecture is quite complex, involving multiple interconnected components. While modularity is a strength, the complexity also raises concerns about the training difficulty and the potential for increased computational cost. The paper claims reduced computational cost, but this would need to be convincingly demonstrated with thorough benchmarking.
    *   **Potential for over-engineering:** Given the combination of different mechanisms and the complexity of the model, there's a risk of over-engineering, where the individual components may not synergistically improve the performance and could possibly hinder performance because of the complexity.
    *   **Lack of quantitative results compared with baselines**: Without comparative results with well-established GAN baselines (e.g., StyleGAN2, StyleGAN3, ProGAN) the claimed improvements in image quality are unsupported. The performance gains need to be rigorously quantified with appropriate metrics to provide a good evaluation.
    *   **Ambiguous datasets.** While datasets such as COCO are easily understood, the provided datasets INKK, AWUN, IONJ, POKL, OPIN are ambiguous. These need to be made clearer.

*   **Significance and Potential Impact:**

    *   If the claimed improvements in image quality, stability, and efficiency are genuinely achieved, MSPG-SEN could contribute meaningfully to the field of generative modeling.
    *   The architecture could serve as a basis for future research on more robust and efficient GAN training techniques.
    *   The modular design and adaptive feedback loop could inspire new approaches for addressing the challenges of GAN training.

*   **Justification of Score:**

    The paper showcases potential by integrating multiple state-of-the-art techniques in GAN research and aims to address critical limitations in GAN training. However, the current presentation in the abstract falls short in providing convincing evidence to support the claims. The lack of concrete performance metrics, specific comparative benchmarks, and clear descriptions of the datasets significantly weakens the assessment of the paper's true novelty and impact. There is limited novelty of the individual components and a lack of quantitative results to validate improvements.

    Therefore, a score of **4** is assigned. This score reflects the potential value of the approach but also acknowledges the significant lack of supporting evidence and the considerable work needed to validate the claims rigorously.

**Score: 4**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 4/10

### CYCLE-INSTRUCT: Fully Seed-Free Instruction Tuning via Dual Self-Training and Cycle Consistency
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.16100v1)
- **Authors**: Zhanming Shen, Hao Chen, Yulei Tang, Shaolin Zhu, Wentao Ye, Xiaomeng Hu, Haobo Wang, Gang Chen, Junbo Zhao
- **Abstract**: Instruction tuning is vital for aligning large language models (LLMs) with human intent, but current methods typically rely on costly human-annotated seed data or powerful external teacher models. While instruction back-translation techniques reduce this dependency, they remain fundamentally tethered to an initial seed set, which limits full automation, introduces biases, and can lead to inefficient use of unlabeled corpora. In this paper, we propose Cycle-Instruct, a novel framework that achieves fully seed-free instruction tuning. Inspired by cycle consistency, Cycle-Instruct employs a dual self-training loop where two models-an answer generator and a question generator-are bootstrapped solely from raw, unlabeled text. These models mutually supervise each other by reconstructing original text segments from their counterpart's generated pseudo-labels, effectively learning from the intrinsic structure of the data without any human-provided seeds. We demonstrate Cycle-Instruct's efficacy across four diverse data tracks, including general instruction-following, domain-specific tasks, dialogue logs, and plain text. Our extensive experiments show that Cycle-Instruct not only outperforms seed-driven back-translation baselines but also achieves performance comparable to strongly supervised methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "CYCLE-INSTRUCT: Fully Seed-Free Instruction Tuning via Dual Self-Training and Cycle Consistency":

**Summary:**

The paper proposes CYCLE-INSTRUCT, a novel framework for instruction tuning large language models (LLMs) *without* relying on human-annotated seed data or external teacher models. The core idea is a dual self-training loop inspired by cycle consistency. Two models, an answer generator and a question generator, are bootstrapped from raw, unlabeled text. These models mutually supervise each other by reconstructing original text segments from the pseudo-labels generated by their counterpart. The reconstruction error serves as the training objective. The authors demonstrate the framework's effectiveness across four different data tracks: general instruction following, domain-specific tasks, dialogue logs, and plain text. The results show that CYCLE-INSTRUCT outperforms seed-driven back-translation baselines and achieves performance comparable to strongly supervised methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in completely eliminating the dependence on seed data in instruction tuning. Previous back-translation techniques still relied on an initial seed set. CYCLE-INSTRUCT's seed-free approach represents a significant step towards automating the instruction tuning process and exploiting the vast amounts of available unlabeled text. The cycle consistency approach in the context of instruction tuning, while inspired by its use in machine translation and image processing, is also a novel adaptation.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from its potential to democratize instruction tuning. Seed data is expensive and often biased.  A seed-free approach makes instruction tuning more accessible, especially for scenarios where labeled data is scarce or unavailable (e.g., privacy-preserving settings, low-resource languages, domain-specific instructions). Furthermore, the cycle-consistency based approach to generating high quality instruction response pairs from raw text in itself is a significant advancement.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Full automation:**  The biggest strength is the complete removal of the seed data bottleneck.
    *   **Strong empirical results:** The paper provides comprehensive experimental results across diverse datasets, demonstrating the effectiveness of CYCLE-INSTRUCT. The comparisons with seed-driven back-translation baselines and even fully supervised methods highlight the framework's potential.
    *   **Clear and well-motivated approach:** The paper clearly explains the methodology and motivates the design choices. The connection to cycle consistency is well-established and the use of a dual self-training loop is intuitive.
    *   **Detailed analysis:** The ablation studies, particularly the analysis of clustered seed selection and the effectiveness of cycle-consistency filtering, provide valuable insights into the framework's behavior.
    *   ** addresses back-translation shortcomings on multi-task instruction augmentation,** such as the ability to infuse task specificity

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on a question mark:** The seed-free segmentation approach depends on the presence of question marks, potentially limiting the framework's applicability to text without explicit interrogatives. This is acknowledged in the limitations section, though.
    *   **Compute-intensive:** The dual self-training loop, while effective, can be computationally expensive due to the need to train two models. It also needs compute for the k-means analysis.
    *   **Limited model scale:** The paper mentions that it relies only on fine tuning using LoRA. This may limits scalability of performance at full-parameter or larger scales.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field by paving the way for more automated and scalable instruction tuning methods. The seed-free approach can encourage exploration of new data sources and enable the creation of more diverse and unbiased instruction datasets. It has a potential application in federated learning contexts.

*  **Technical soundness:** While the concept is grounded and logical, the claim about full removal of seed data may need to be qualified to account for human design of model and algorithm, and for human selection of raw data. This is minor.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of instruction tuning.  The fully seed-free approach is a substantial advancement that addresses a major bottleneck in existing methods.  The strong empirical results across diverse datasets demonstrate the framework's effectiveness. The paper is well-written and provides a clear and detailed explanation of the methodology.

The weaknesses, such as the reliance on question marks in data segmentation and the compute intensity, are acknowledged and do not significantly detract from the overall contribution.  Although cycle consistency has been used elsewhere, its application to instruction tuning within this dual self-training framework is novel and effective. Given the potential impact of this work on democratizing instruction tuning and enabling the exploitation of vast amounts of unlabeled data, a score of 8 is warranted.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Extending FKG.in: Towards a Food Claim Traceability Network
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.16117v1)
- **Authors**: Saransh Kumar Gupta, Rizwan Gulzar Mir, Lipika Dey, Partha Pratim Das, Anirban Sen, Ramesh Jain
- **Abstract**: The global food landscape is rife with scientific, cultural, and commercial claims about what foods are, what they do, what they should not do, or should not do. These range from rigorously studied health benefits (probiotics improve gut health) and misrepresentations (soaked almonds make one smarter) to vague promises (superfoods boost immunity) and culturally rooted beliefs (cold foods cause coughs). Despite their widespread influence, the infrastructure for tracing, verifying, and contextualizing these claims remains fragmented and underdeveloped. In this paper, we propose a Food Claim-Traceability Network (FCN) as an extension of FKG.in, a knowledge graph of Indian food that we have been incrementally building. We also present the ontology design and the semi-automated knowledge curation workflow that we used to develop a proof of concept of FKG.in-FCN using Reddit data and Large Language Models. FCN integrates curated data inputs, structured schemas, and provenance-aware pipelines for food-related claim extraction and validation. While directly linked to the Indian food knowledge graph as an application, our methodology remains application-agnostic and adaptable to other geographic, culinary, or regulatory settings. By modeling food claims and their traceability in a structured, verifiable, and explainable way, we aim to contribute to more transparent and accountable food knowledge ecosystems, supporting researchers, policymakers, and most importantly, everyday consumers in navigating a world saturated with dietary assertions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a Food Claim-Traceability Network (FCN) as an extension of FKG.in, a knowledge graph of Indian food. The FCN aims to capture, validate, and trace food-related claims across various sources (Reddit, scientific papers, etc.), focusing on Indian food initially but designed for broader applicability.  The authors outline the ontology design, semi-automated knowledge curation workflow using Reddit data and Large Language Models (LLMs), and present a proof-of-concept implementation. The key focus is on structuring food claims as knowledge artifacts, linking them to relevant food items, and tracking their origins, transformations, and validations.  This addresses the current fragmented infrastructure for tracing and verifying such claims in a world saturated with dietary assertions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty lies in the **integrated approach** to address food claim traceability, incorporating a combination of:
    *   Knowledge Graph extension.
    *   Ontology design specifically for food claims (beyond nutrition facts).
    *   Semi-automated extraction and validation using LLMs.
    *   Emphasis on cultural context and diverse sources (including informal ones).
    While individual components (food ontologies, claim verification, LLM-based extraction) exist, their synthesis within the Indian food context and the clear focus on *traceability* and cultural nuance are significant.
    A limitation to this is a strong similarity to previous research on scientific claim verification. The main new aspect of the research here is a application to nutrition, in addition to using Large Language Models, which have seen a significant increase in usage the past few years.

*   **Significance:** The significance is substantial, particularly within the context of India:
    *   Addresses a critical need to combat food misinformation, especially given the diverse cultural and linguistic landscape.
    *   Provides a framework for regulatory bodies (like FSSAI) to verify claims.
    *   Empowers consumers with evidence-based information, improving health literacy.
    *   Has broader implications for other regions facing similar challenges with dietary misinformation.
    It's important to note that the described system is a significant effort, which requires extensive time, knowledge, and maintenance to properly train, test, and update.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation, especially regarding the challenges specific to India.
    *   Well-defined ontology design, considering diverse aspects of food claims (semantic, contextual, validity).
    *   Modular and adaptable workflow, incorporating LLMs and human validation.
    *   Proof-of-concept demonstration showcasing the feasibility of the approach.
    *   Comprehensive literature review, covering relevant work in food ontologies, knowledge graphs, and claim verification.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited scale of the proof-of-concept (small Reddit dataset).
    *   Reliance on LLMs, which can be prone to hallucinations and require careful prompt engineering.
    *   The current implementation is not yet grounded in Indian food claims specifically; this is a key future step.
    *   Potential scalability challenges with human-in-the-loop validation for a larger and more diverse dataset.
    *   The paper lacks a thorough comparison against existing scientific claim verification systems.
    *   Difficult to ensure source credibility in an automated approach, which can cause issues.

*   **Impact:** The potential impact is high. If successfully scaled and implemented, the FCN could become a valuable resource for researchers, policymakers, and consumers in navigating the complex world of dietary information. The emphasis on traceability and cultural context is crucial for ensuring that food knowledge is both accurate and relevant.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel synthesis of existing techniques to address a significant real-world problem. The emphasis on traceability, cultural context, and the potential for empowering consumers in a misinformation-rich environment is commendable. However, the limited scale of the proof-of-concept and the reliance on LLMs with their associated limitations constrain the immediate impact. Further, it lacks a strong comparison to other verification systems, leading to a slightly lower score.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Text Takes Over: A Study of Modality Bias in Multimodal Intent Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.16122v1)
- **Authors**: Ankan Mullick, Saransh Sharma, Abhik Jana, Pawan Goyal
- **Abstract**: The rise of multimodal data, integrating text, audio, and visuals, has created new opportunities for studying multimodal tasks such as intent detection. This work investigates the effectiveness of Large Language Models (LLMs) and non-LLMs, including text-only and multi-modal models, in the multimodal intent detection task. Our study reveals that Mistral-7B, a text-only LLM, outperforms most competitive multimodal models by approximately 9% on MIntRec-1 and 4% on MIntRec2.0 datasets. This performance advantage comes from a strong textual bias in these datasets, where over 90% of the samples require textual input, either alone or in combination with other modalities, for correct classification. We confirm the modality bias of these datasets via human evaluation, too. Next, we propose a framework to debias the datasets, and upon debiasing, more than 70% of the samples in MIntRec-1 and more than 50% in MIntRec2.0 get removed, resulting in significant performance degradation across all models, with smaller multimodal fusion models being the most affected with an accuracy drop of over 50 - 60%. Further, we analyze the context-specific relevance of different modalities through empirical analysis. Our findings highlight the challenges posed by modality bias in multimodal intent datasets and emphasize the need for unbiased datasets to evaluate multimodal models effectively.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates modality bias in multimodal intent detection datasets. The authors find that existing datasets, namely MIntRec-1 and MIntRec2.0, are heavily biased towards textual input. A text-only LLM (Mistral-7B) outperforms multimodal models on these datasets, suggesting that multimodal inputs are often unnecessary. Through human evaluation and an automated debiasing framework, they identify and remove biased samples, leading to a significant performance drop across all models, especially smaller multimodal models. The paper also explores context-specific relevancy of modalities and highlights the need for unbiased datasets for effective evaluation of multimodal intent detection models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary contribution lies in its systematic analysis of modality bias in popular multimodal intent detection datasets. While modality bias has been observed in other multimodal tasks, its thorough investigation within intent detection is a valuable contribution.  The debiasing framework is a practical tool that can be applied to other datasets as well. While not revolutionary, the analysis is comprehensive and insightful. The use of relatively recent and high-performing models such as Mistral-7B and recent LLMs to showcase the effect is commendable.

*   **Significance:**  The paper's findings are significant for the multimodal intent detection community. It challenges the prevailing assumption that simply incorporating more modalities automatically leads to better performance. It demonstrates that biased datasets can lead to inflated performance metrics and misguide model development efforts. The proposed debiasing framework provides a practical solution for creating more balanced datasets. Ultimately, the work advocates for a more critical evaluation of multimodal models and emphasizes the importance of dataset quality.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Comprehensive Evaluation: The paper conducts a thorough evaluation using a diverse set of models (LLMs and non-LLMs).
    *   Practical Debiasing Framework: The proposed framework is a valuable contribution for creating balanced datasets.
    *   Human Evaluation:  The paper validates the findings with human annotations, strengthening the claims.
    *   Clear Presentation:  The paper is well-written and clearly presents the experimental setup, results, and analysis.
    *   Relevance: The focus on practical datasets (MIntRec) adds relevance.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited Generalizability: The analysis is focused on two specific datasets. While the findings are likely applicable to other datasets, this needs to be explicitly validated.
    *   Complexity of Debiasing: Debiasing inherently involves subjective judgment, and while the paper tries to automate the process, human biases could still influence the removal of samples. The reliance on specific models (BERT, Llama, SDIF) to identify bias could also introduce biases specific to these models.
    *   Loss of Data: The debiasing process leads to a substantial reduction in dataset size, which could hinder the training of more complex models that require large amounts of data. The reduced dataset size, while less biased, might not be representative enough for practical applications.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence the way researchers evaluate multimodal intent detection models. It highlights the need for creating and using unbiased datasets and motivates the development of more robust fusion techniques that are less susceptible to modality bias. It could also drive research into more sophisticated debiasing techniques.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7** to this paper.

*   The paper's analysis of modality bias is novel and significant within the specific context of multimodal intent detection. The proposed debiasing framework is a valuable tool. The paper is well-executed and clearly presented. However, the limitations in terms of generalizability and the potential for bias in the debiasing process, and the considerable reduction in dataset size prevent a higher score.
